					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content mw-content-ltr" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="mw-parser-output"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">Christian saint and martyr (died 303)</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">"St. George" redirects here. For other uses, see <a href="/wiki/St._George_(disambiguation)" class="mw-redirect mw-disambig" title="St. George (disambiguation)">St. George (disambiguation)</a> and <a href="/wiki/Saint_George_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Saint George (disambiguation)">Saint George (disambiguation)</a>.</div>
<p class="mw-empty-elt">

</p>

</div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Saint_symbolism" title="Saint symbolism">Attributes</a></th><td class="infobox-data">Clothed as a <a href="/wiki/Crusades" title="Crusades">crusader</a> in <a href="/wiki/Plate_armour" title="Plate armour">plate armour</a> or <a href="/wiki/Mail_(armour)" class="mw-redirect" title="Mail (armour)">mail</a>, often bearing a <a href="/wiki/Lance" title="Lance">lance</a> tipped by a cross, riding a white horse, often slaying a <a href="/wiki/Dragon" title="Dragon">dragon</a>. In the <a href="/wiki/Greek_East_and_Latin_West" title="Greek East and Latin West">Greek East and Latin West</a> he is shown with <a href="/wiki/St_George%27s_Cross" class="mw-redirect" title="St George&#39;s Cross">St George's Cross</a> emblazoned on his armour, or <a href="/wiki/Shield" title="Shield">shield</a> or <a href="/wiki/Banner" title="Banner">banner</a>.</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">Patronage</a></th><td class="infobox-data">Many <a href="/wiki/Patronages_of_Saint_George" title="Patronages of Saint George">patronages of Saint George</a> exist around the world</td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Saint George</b> (<a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a>: <span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language text"><span lang="grc">Γεώργιος</span></span>, <small><a href="/wiki/Romanization_of_Greek" title="Romanization of Greek">translit.</a></small>&#160;<span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language romanization"><i lang="grc-Latn"><i>Geṓrgios</i></i></span>, <a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Geōrgius</i>, <a href="/wiki/Georgian_language" title="Georgian language">Georgian</a>: გიორგი, <a href="/wiki/Transliteration" title="Transliteration">translit.</a> Giorgi,  <a href="/wiki/Syriac_language" title="Syriac language">Syriac</a>: <span lang="syr" dir="rtl">ܡܪܝ ܓܘܪܓܝܣ</span>, <small><a href="/wiki/Romanization_of_Syriac" class="mw-redirect" title="Romanization of Syriac">romanized</a>:&#160;</small><span title="Syriac-language romanization"><i lang="syr-Latn">Mar Giwargis</i></span> <a href="/wiki/Arabic_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Arabic language">Arabic</a>: <span lang="ar" dir="rtl">قديس جرجي</span>, <small><a href="/wiki/Romanization_of_Arabic" title="Romanization of Arabic">romanized</a>:&#160;</small><span title="Arabic-language romanization"><i lang="ar-Latn">Qiddīs Jurjī</i></span>; died 23 April 303), also <b>George of Lydda</b>, was a <a href="/wiki/Christians" title="Christians">Christian</a> who is venerated as a <a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">saint</a> in Christianity. According to tradition, he was a soldier in the <a href="/wiki/Roman_army" title="Roman army">Roman army</a>. He was of <a href="/wiki/Cappadocian_Greeks" title="Cappadocian Greeks">Cappadocian Greek</a> origin and a member of the <a href="/wiki/Praetorian_Guard" title="Praetorian Guard">Praetorian Guard</a> for <a href="/wiki/Roman_emperor" title="Roman emperor">Roman emperor</a> <a href="/wiki/Diocletian" title="Diocletian">Diocletian</a>, but was sentenced to death for refusing to recant his Christian faith. He became one of the most <a href="/wiki/Saint_George_in_devotions,_traditions_and_prayers" title="Saint George in devotions, traditions and prayers">venerated</a> saints and <a href="/wiki/Great_martyr" title="Great martyr">megalomartyrs</a> in Christianity, and he has been especially venerated as a <a href="/wiki/Military_saint" title="Military saint">military saint</a> since the <a href="/wiki/Crusader_States" class="mw-redirect" title="Crusader States">Crusades</a>. He is respected by Christians, Druze, as well as some Muslims as a martyr of monotheistic faith.
</p><p>In <a href="/wiki/Hagiography" title="Hagiography">hagiography</a>, as one of the <a href="/wiki/Fourteen_Holy_Helpers" title="Fourteen Holy Helpers">Fourteen Holy Helpers</a> and one of the most prominent military saints, he is immortalized in the legend of <a href="/wiki/Saint_George_and_the_Dragon" title="Saint George and the Dragon">Saint George and the Dragon</a>. His memorial, <a href="/wiki/Saint_George%27s_Day" title="Saint George&#39;s Day">Saint George's Day</a>, is traditionally celebrated on 23 April. Historically, the countries of England, Ukraine, Ethiopia, and Georgia, as well as <a href="/wiki/Catalonia" title="Catalonia">Catalonia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Aragon" title="Aragon">Aragon</a> in Spain, and Moscow in Russia, have claimed George as their <a href="/wiki/Patronages_of_Saint_George" title="Patronages of Saint George">patron</a> saint, as have several other regions, cities, universities, professions, and organizations. The <a href="/wiki/Church_of_Saint_George_and_Mosque_of_Al-Khadr" class="mw-redirect" title="Church of Saint George and Mosque of Al-Khadr">Church of Saint George and Mosque of Al-Khadr</a> in <a href="/wiki/Lod" title="Lod">Lod</a> (Lydda), Israel, contains a sarcophagus believed by many Christians to contain St. George's remains.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legend">Legend</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Legend">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Very little is known about George's life, but it is thought he was a <a href="/wiki/Roman_Empire" title="Roman Empire">Roman</a> officer of <a href="/wiki/Greeks" title="Greeks">Greek</a> descent who was martyred in one of the <a href="/wiki/Persecution_of_Christians_in_the_Roman_Empire" title="Persecution of Christians in the Roman Empire">pre-Constantinian persecutions</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> Beyond this, early sources give conflicting information.
</p><p>The saint's veneration dates to the 5th century with some certainty, and possibly even to the 4th. The addition of the <a href="/wiki/Saint_George_and_the_Dragon" title="Saint George and the Dragon">dragon legend</a> dates to the 11th century.
</p><p>The earliest text which preserves fragments of George's narrative is in a Greek hagiography which is identified by <a href="/wiki/Hippolyte_Delehaye" title="Hippolyte Delehaye">Hippolyte Delehaye</a> of the scholarly <a href="/wiki/Bollandist" title="Bollandist">Bollandists</a> to be a <a href="/wiki/Palimpsest" title="Palimpsest">palimpsest</a> of the 5th century.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> An earlier work by <a href="/wiki/Eusebius" title="Eusebius">Eusebius</a>, <i><a href="/wiki/Church_History_(Eusebius)" title="Church History (Eusebius)">Church history</a></i>, written in the 4th century, contributed to the legend but did not name George or provide significant detail.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> The work of the Bollandists <a href="/wiki/Daniel_Papebroch" title="Daniel Papebroch">Daniel Papebroch</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jean_Bolland" title="Jean Bolland">Jean Bolland</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Godfrey_Henschen" title="Godfrey Henschen">Godfrey Henschen</a> in the 17th century was one of the first pieces of scholarly research to establish the saint's historicity, via their publications in <i><a href="/wiki/Bibliotheca_Hagiographica_Graeca" title="Bibliotheca Hagiographica Graeca">Bibliotheca Hagiographica Graeca</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Pope_Gelasius_I" title="Pope Gelasius I">Pope Gelasius I</a> stated in 494 that George was among those saints "whose names are justly reverenced among men, but whose actions are known only to God."<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The most complete version, based upon the fifth-century Greek text but in a later form, survives in a translation into <a href="/wiki/Syriac_language" title="Syriac language">Syriac</a> from about 600. From text fragments preserved in the <a href="/wiki/British_Library" title="British Library">British Library</a>, a translation into English was published in 1925.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the Greek tradition, George was born to Greek Christian parents, in <a href="/wiki/Cappadocia_(Roman_province)" title="Cappadocia (Roman province)">Cappadocia</a>. After his father died, his mother, who was originally from <a href="/wiki/Lod" title="Lod">Lydda</a>, in <a href="/wiki/Syria_Palaestina" title="Syria Palaestina">Syria Palaestina</a>, returned with George to her hometown.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> He went on to become a soldier for the <a href="/wiki/Roman_army" title="Roman army">Roman army</a>; but, because of his <a href="/wiki/Christians" title="Christians">Christian</a> faith, he was arrested and tortured, "at or near Lydda, also called <a href="/wiki/Lod" title="Lod">Diospolis</a>"; on the following day, he was paraded and then beheaded, and his body was buried in Lydda.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_15-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> According to other sources, after his mother's death, George travelled to the eastern imperial capital, <a href="/wiki/Nicomedia" title="Nicomedia">Nicomedia</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> where he was persecuted by one <i>Dadianus</i>. In later versions of the Greek legend, this name is rationalised to <a href="/wiki/Diocletian" title="Diocletian">Diocletian</a>, and George's martyrdom is placed in the <a href="/wiki/Diocletianic_Persecution" title="Diocletianic Persecution">Diocletian persecution</a> of AD 303. The setting in Nicomedia is also secondary, and inconsistent with the earliest cults of the saint being located in <a href="/wiki/Lod" title="Lod">Diospolis</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-CathEnc_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CathEnc-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>
George was executed by <a href="/wiki/Decapitation" title="Decapitation">decapitation</a> on 23 April 303. A witness of his suffering convinced Empress <a href="/wiki/Alexandra_of_Rome" title="Alexandra of Rome">Alexandra of Rome</a> to become a Christian as well, so she joined George in martyrdom. His body was buried in <a href="/wiki/Lod" title="Lod">Lydda</a>, where Christians soon came to honour him as a martyr.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Butler_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Butler-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup></p><figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Saint_George_in_the_Acta_Sanctorum.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Saint_George_in_the_Acta_Sanctorum.png/220px-Saint_George_in_the_Acta_Sanctorum.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="110" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Saint_George_in_the_Acta_Sanctorum.png/330px-Saint_George_in_the_Acta_Sanctorum.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Saint_George_in_the_Acta_Sanctorum.png/440px-Saint_George_in_the_Acta_Sanctorum.png 2x" data-file-width="1115" data-file-height="556" /></a><figcaption>George in the <i><a href="/wiki/Acta_Sanctorum" title="Acta Sanctorum">Acta Sanctorum</a></i>, as collected in late 1600s and early 1700s. The Latin title <i>De S Georgio Megalo-Martyre; Lyddae seu Diospoli in Palaestina</i> translates as <i>St. George Great-Martyr; [from] <a href="/wiki/Lod" title="Lod">Lydda</a> or Diospolis, in Palestine</i>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The Latin <i>Passio Sancti Georgii</i> (6th century) follows the general course of the Greek legend, but Diocletian here becomes <i>Dacian, Emperor of the Persians</i>. His martyrdom was greatly extended to more than twenty separate tortures over the course of seven years. Over the course of his martyrdom, 40,900 pagans were converted to Christianity, including the Empress Alexandra. When George finally died, the wicked Dacian was carried away in a whirlwind of fire. In later Latin versions, the persecutor is the Roman emperor <a href="/wiki/Decius" title="Decius">Decius</a>, or a Roman judge named <a href="/wiki/Dacian_(prefect)" title="Dacian (prefect)">Dacian</a> serving under Diocletian.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Historicity">Historicity</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Historicity">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Nuremberg_chronicles_f_124v_2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/Nuremberg_chronicles_f_124v_2.jpg/220px-Nuremberg_chronicles_f_124v_2.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="310" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/2/2b/Nuremberg_chronicles_f_124v_2.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="227" data-file-height="320" /></a><figcaption>George depicted in the <i><a href="/wiki/Nuremberg_Chronicle" title="Nuremberg Chronicle">Nuremberg Chronicle</a></i> of 1493</figcaption></figure>
<p>There is little information on the early life of George. <a href="/wiki/Herbert_Thurston" title="Herbert Thurston">Herbert Thurston</a> in <i><a href="/wiki/The_Catholic_Encyclopedia" class="mw-redirect" title="The Catholic Encyclopedia">The Catholic Encyclopedia</a></i> states that, based upon an ancient <a href="/wiki/Cult_(religious_practice)" title="Cult (religious practice)">cultus</a>, narratives of the early pilgrims, and the early dedications of churches to George, going back to the fourth century, "there seems, therefore, no ground for doubting the historical existence of St. George", although no faith can be placed in either the details of his history or his alleged exploits.<sup id="cite_ref-CE1913_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CE1913-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:S.George_(Novgorod,_mid._14_c,_GTG).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4e/S.George_%28Novgorod%2C_mid._14_c%2C_GTG%29.jpg/170px-S.George_%28Novgorod%2C_mid._14_c%2C_GTG%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="256" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4e/S.George_%28Novgorod%2C_mid._14_c%2C_GTG%29.jpg/255px-S.George_%28Novgorod%2C_mid._14_c%2C_GTG%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4e/S.George_%28Novgorod%2C_mid._14_c%2C_GTG%29.jpg/340px-S.George_%28Novgorod%2C_mid._14_c%2C_GTG%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="399" data-file-height="600" /></a><figcaption>Russian icon (mid 14th century), <a href="/wiki/Novgorod_Republic" title="Novgorod Republic">Novgorod</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Diocletianic_Persecution" title="Diocletianic Persecution">Diocletianic Persecution</a> of 303, associated with <a href="/wiki/Military_saint" title="Military saint">military saints</a> because the persecution was aimed at Christians among the professional soldiers of the <a href="/wiki/Roman_army" title="Roman army">Roman army</a>, is of undisputed historicity. According to <a href="/wiki/Donald_Attwater" title="Donald Attwater">Donald Attwater</a>,
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>No historical particulars of his life have survived,&#160;... The widespread veneration for St George as a soldier saint from early times had its centre in Palestine at Diospolis, now <a href="/wiki/Lod" title="Lod">Lydda</a>. St George was apparently martyred there, at the end of the third or the beginning of the fourth century; that is all that can be reasonably surmised about him.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p><a href="/wiki/Edward_Gibbon" title="Edward Gibbon">Edward Gibbon</a><sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Emerson_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Emerson-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> argued that George, or at least the legend from which the above is distilled, is based on <a href="/wiki/George_of_Cappadocia" title="George of Cappadocia">George of Cappadocia</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Gibbon_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gibbon-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-CathEnc_17-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CathEnc-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> a notorious 4th-century Arian bishop who was <a href="/wiki/Athanasius_of_Alexandria" title="Athanasius of Alexandria">Athanasius of Alexandria</a>'s most bitter rival, and that it was he who in time became George of England. This identification is seen as highly improbable. Bishop George was slain by Gentile Greeks for exacting onerous taxes, especially inheritance taxes. <a href="/wiki/J._B._Bury" title="J. B. Bury">J. B. Bury</a>, who edited the 1906 edition of Gibbon's <i>The Decline and Fall</i>, wrote "this theory of Gibbon's has nothing to be said for it". He adds that "the connection of St. George with a dragon-slaying legend does not relegate him to the region of the myth".<sup id="cite_ref-CE1913_21-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CE1913-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> Saint George in all likelihood was martyred before the year 290.<sup id="cite_ref-hogg_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-hogg-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="St._George_and_the_dragon">St. George and the dragon</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: St. George and the dragon">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Saint_George_and_the_Dragon" title="Saint George and the Dragon">Saint George and the Dragon</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:St_George_and_the_Dragon_Verona_ms_1853_26r.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7c/St_George_and_the_Dragon_Verona_ms_1853_26r.jpg/220px-St_George_and_the_Dragon_Verona_ms_1853_26r.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="217" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7c/St_George_and_the_Dragon_Verona_ms_1853_26r.jpg/330px-St_George_and_the_Dragon_Verona_ms_1853_26r.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7c/St_George_and_the_Dragon_Verona_ms_1853_26r.jpg/440px-St_George_and_the_Dragon_Verona_ms_1853_26r.jpg 2x" data-file-width="868" data-file-height="855" /></a><figcaption>Miniature from a 13th-century <i>Passio Sancti Georgii</i> (<a href="/wiki/Verona" title="Verona">Verona</a>)</figcaption></figure>
<p>The earliest known record of the legend of <a href="/wiki/Saint_George_and_the_Dragon" title="Saint George and the Dragon">Saint George and the Dragon</a> occurs in the 11th-century, in a Georgian source.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> It reached Catholic Europe in the 12th-century. In the <i><a href="/wiki/Golden_Legend" title="Golden Legend">Golden Legend</a></i>, by 13th-century Archbishop of <a href="/wiki/Genoa" title="Genoa">Genoa</a> <a href="/wiki/Jacobus_de_Voragine" title="Jacobus de Voragine">Jacobus de Voragine</a>, George's death was at the hands of <a href="/wiki/Dacian_(prefect)" title="Dacian (prefect)">Dacian</a>, and about the year 287.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Martorell_-_Sant_Jordi.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/Martorell_-_Sant_Jordi.jpg/180px-Martorell_-_Sant_Jordi.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="289" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/Martorell_-_Sant_Jordi.jpg/270px-Martorell_-_Sant_Jordi.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/Martorell_-_Sant_Jordi.jpg/360px-Martorell_-_Sant_Jordi.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1401" data-file-height="2250" /></a><figcaption><i><a href="/wiki/Saint_George_Killing_the_Dragon" title="Saint George Killing the Dragon">Saint George Killing the Dragon</a>,</i> 1434/35, by <a href="/wiki/Bernat_Martorell" title="Bernat Martorell">Bernat Martorell</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>The tradition tells that a fierce dragon was causing panic at the city of Silene, <a href="/wiki/Libya" title="Libya">Libya</a>, at the time George arrived there. In order to prevent the dragon from devastating people from the city, they gave two sheep each day to the dragon, but when the sheep were not enough they were forced to sacrifice humans instead of the two sheep. The human to be sacrificed was elected by the city's own people and one time the king's daughter was chosen to be sacrificed but no one was willing to take her place. George saved the girl by slaying the dragon with a lance. The king was so grateful that he offered him treasures as a reward for saving his daughter's life, but George refused it and instead he gave these to the poor. The people of the city were so amazed at what they had witnessed that they became Christians and were all baptized.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <i>Golden Legend</i> offered a narration of George's encounter with a <a href="/wiki/Dragon" title="Dragon">dragon</a>. This account was very influential and it remains the most familiar version in English owing to <a href="/wiki/William_Caxton" title="William Caxton">William Caxton</a>'s 15th-century translation.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the medieval romances, the lance with which George slew the dragon was called Ascalon, after the <a href="/wiki/Levant" title="Levant">Levantine</a> city of <a href="/wiki/Ashkelon" title="Ashkelon">Ashkelon</a>, today in Israel. The name <i>Ascalon</i> was used by <a href="/wiki/Winston_Churchill" title="Winston Churchill">Winston Churchill</a> for his personal aircraft during <a href="/wiki/World_War_II" title="World War II">World War II</a>, according to records at <a href="/wiki/Bletchley_Park" title="Bletchley Park">Bletchley Park</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> Iconography of the horseman with spear overcoming evil was widespread throughout the Christian period.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Muslim_legends">Muslim legends</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Muslim legends">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>George (<a href="/wiki/Arabic_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Arabic language">Arabic</a>: <span lang="ar" dir="rtl">جرجس</span>, <i>Jirjis</i> or <i>Girgus</i>) is included in some Muslim texts as a prophetic figure. The Islamic sources state that he lived among a group of believers who were in direct contact with the last <a href="/wiki/Apostles_of_Jesus" class="mw-redirect" title="Apostles of Jesus">apostles of Jesus</a>. He is described as a rich merchant who opposed erection of <a href="/wiki/Apollo" title="Apollo">Apollo</a>'s statue by <a href="/wiki/Mosul" title="Mosul">Mosul</a>'s king Dadan. After confronting the king, George was tortured many times to no effect, was imprisoned and was aided by the angels. Eventually, he exposed that the idols were possessed by Satan, but was martyred when the city was destroyed by God in a rain of fire.<sup id="cite_ref-Littlefield_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Littlefield-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Muslim scholars had tried to find a historical connection of the saint due to his popularity.<sup id="cite_ref-Numen_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Numen-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> According to Muslim legend, he was martyred under the rule of <a href="/wiki/Diocletian" title="Diocletian">Diocletian</a> and was killed three times but resurrected every time. The legend is more developed in the Persian version of <a href="/wiki/Al-Tabari" title="Al-Tabari">al-Tabari</a> wherein he resurrects the dead, makes trees sprout and pillars bear flowers. After one of his deaths, the world is covered by darkness which is lifted only when he is resurrected. He is able to convert the queen but she is put to death. He then prays to God to allow him to die, which is granted.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Al-Tha%CA%BFlabi" class="mw-redirect" title="Al-Thaʿlabi">Al-Thaʿlabi</a> states that he was from Palestine and lived in the times of some <a href="/wiki/Disciples_of_Jesus_in_Islam" title="Disciples of Jesus in Islam">disciples of Jesus</a>. He was killed many times by the king of Mosul, and resurrected each time. When the king tried to starve him, he touched a piece of dry wood brought by a woman and turned it green, with varieties of fruits and vegetables growing from it. After his fourth death, the city was burnt along with him. <a href="/wiki/Ibn_al-Athir" title="Ibn al-Athir">Ibn al-Athir</a>'s account of one of his deaths is parallel to the <a href="/wiki/Crucifixion_of_Jesus" title="Crucifixion of Jesus">crucifixion of Jesus</a>, stating, "When he died, God sent stormy winds and thunder and lightning and dark clouds, so that darkness fell between heaven and earth, and people were in great wonderment." The account adds that the darkness was lifted after his resurrection.<sup id="cite_ref-Numen_33-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Numen-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Veneration">Veneration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Veneration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Saint_George_in_devotions,_traditions_and_prayers" title="Saint George in devotions, traditions and prayers">Saint George in devotions, traditions and prayers</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="History">History</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: History">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Paolo_Veronese_023.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Paolo_Veronese_023.jpg/220px-Paolo_Veronese_023.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="306" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Paolo_Veronese_023.jpg/330px-Paolo_Veronese_023.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Paolo_Veronese_023.jpg/440px-Paolo_Veronese_023.jpg 2x" data-file-width="900" data-file-height="1251" /></a><figcaption><i>Martyrdom of Saint George</i>, by <a href="/wiki/Paolo_Veronese" title="Paolo Veronese">Paolo Veronese</a>, 1564</figcaption></figure>
<p>A <a href="/wiki/Titular_church" title="Titular church">titular church</a> built in Lydda during the reign of <a href="/wiki/Constantine_the_Great" title="Constantine the Great">Constantine the Great</a> (reigned 306–337) was consecrated to "a man of the highest distinction", according to the church history of <a href="/wiki/Eusebius" title="Eusebius">Eusebius</a>; the name of the <i>titulus</i> "patron" was not disclosed, but later he was asserted<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Words_to_watch#Unsupported_attributions" title="Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Words to watch"><span title="The material near this tag may use weasel words or too-vague attribution. (June 2017)">by whom?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> to have been George.
</p><p>The veneration of George spread from <a href="/wiki/Syria_Palaestina" title="Syria Palaestina">Syria Palaestina</a> through Lebanon to the rest of the <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Empire" title="Byzantine Empire">Byzantine Empire</a> – though the martyr is not mentioned in the Syriac <i><a href="/wiki/Horologion" title="Horologion">Breviarium</a></i><sup id="cite_ref-Butler_19-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Butler-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> – and the region <a href="/wiki/Georgia_(country)" title="Georgia (country)">east of the Black Sea</a>. By the 5th century, the veneration of George had reached the Christian <a href="/wiki/Western_Roman_Empire" title="Western Roman Empire">Western Roman Empire</a>, as well: in 494, George was canonized as a <a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">saint</a> by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Gelasius_I" title="Pope Gelasius I">Pope Gelasius I</a>, among those "whose names are justly reverenced among men, but whose acts are known only to [God]."<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2017)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>The early cult of the saint was localized in <a href="/wiki/Lod" title="Lod">Diospolis (Lydda)</a>, in Palestine. The first description of Lydda as a pilgrimage site where George's relics were venerated is <i><a href="/wiki/De_Situ_Terrae_Sanctae" class="mw-redirect" title="De Situ Terrae Sanctae">De Situ Terrae Sanctae</a></i> by the archdeacon Theodosius, written between 518 and 530. By the end of the 6th century, the center of his veneration appears to have shifted to <a href="/wiki/Cappadocia_(Roman_province)" title="Cappadocia (Roman province)">Cappadocia</a>. The <i>Life</i> of <a href="/wiki/Saint_Theodore_of_Sykeon" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Theodore of Sykeon">Saint Theodore of Sykeon</a>, written in the 7th century, mentions the veneration of the relics of the saint in Cappadocia.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By the time of the <a href="/wiki/Early_Muslim_conquests" title="Early Muslim conquests">early Muslim conquests</a> of the mostly Christian and <a href="/wiki/Zoroastrianism" title="Zoroastrianism">Zoroastrian</a> Middle East, a <a href="/wiki/Church_of_Saint_George_and_Mosque_of_Al-Khadr" class="mw-redirect" title="Church of Saint George and Mosque of Al-Khadr">basilica in Lydda</a> dedicated to George existed.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> A new church was erected in 1872 and is still standing, where the feast of the translation of the relics of Saint George to that location is celebrated on 3 November each year.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> In England, he was mentioned among the martyrs by the 8th-century monk <a href="/wiki/Bede" title="Bede">Bede</a>. The <i><a href="/wiki/Georgslied" title="Georgslied">Georgslied</a></i> is an adaptation of his legend in <a href="/wiki/Old_High_German" title="Old High German">Old High German</a>, composed in the late 9th century. The earliest dedication to the saint in England is a church at <a href="/wiki/Fordington,_Dorset" title="Fordington, Dorset">Fordington, Dorset</a>, that is mentioned in the will of <a href="/wiki/Alfred_the_Great" title="Alfred the Great">Alfred the Great</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> George did not rise to the position of "patron saint" of England, however, until the 14th century, and he was still obscured by <a href="/wiki/Edward_the_Confessor" title="Edward the Confessor">Edward the Confessor</a>, the traditional patron saint of England, until in 1552 during the reign of <a href="/wiki/Edward_VI" title="Edward VI">Edward VI</a> all saints' banners other than George's were abolished in the <a href="/wiki/English_Reformation" title="English Reformation">English Reformation</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Cornelis_Schut_-_The_beheading_of_Saint_George.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/09/Cornelis_Schut_-_The_beheading_of_Saint_George.jpg/220px-Cornelis_Schut_-_The_beheading_of_Saint_George.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="283" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/09/Cornelis_Schut_-_The_beheading_of_Saint_George.jpg/330px-Cornelis_Schut_-_The_beheading_of_Saint_George.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/09/Cornelis_Schut_-_The_beheading_of_Saint_George.jpg/440px-Cornelis_Schut_-_The_beheading_of_Saint_George.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5170" data-file-height="6659" /></a><figcaption>The martyrdom of Saint George, by <a href="/wiki/Cornelis_Schut" title="Cornelis Schut">Cornelis Schut</a>, 1643</figcaption></figure>
<p>Belief in an apparition of George heartened the <a href="/wiki/Franks" title="Franks">Franks</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Antioch_(1098)" title="Battle of Antioch (1098)">Battle of Antioch</a> in 1098,<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> and a similar appearance occurred the following year at Jerusalem. The chivalric military <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Sant_Jordi_d%27Alfama" title="Order of Sant Jordi d&#39;Alfama">Order of Sant Jordi d'Alfama</a> was established by king <a href="/wiki/Peter_II_of_Aragon" title="Peter II of Aragon">Peter the Catholic</a> from the <a href="/wiki/Crown_of_Aragon" title="Crown of Aragon">Crown of Aragon</a> in 1201, <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Genoa" title="Republic of Genoa">Republic of Genoa</a>, <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Saint_George_(Kingdom_of_Hungary)" title="Order of Saint George (Kingdom of Hungary)">Kingdom of Hungary</a> (1326), and by <a href="/wiki/Frederick_III,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Frederick III, Holy Roman Emperor">Frederick III, Holy Roman Emperor</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Edward_III_of_England" title="Edward III of England">Edward III of England</a> put his <a href="/wiki/Order_of_the_Garter" title="Order of the Garter">Order of the Garter</a> under the banner of George, probably in 1348. The chronicler <a href="/wiki/Jean_Froissart" title="Jean Froissart">Jean Froissart</a> observed the English invoking George as a battle cry on several occasions during the <a href="/wiki/Hundred_Years%27_War" title="Hundred Years&#39; War">Hundred Years' War</a>. In his rise as a national saint, George was aided by the very fact that the saint had no legendary connection with England, and no specifically localised shrine, as that of <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Becket" title="Thomas Becket">Thomas Becket</a> at Canterbury: "Consequently, numerous shrines were established during the late fifteenth century," Muriel C. McClendon has written,<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> "and his did not become closely identified with a particular occupation or with the cure of a specific malady."
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Igreja_Matriz_de_S%C3%A3o_Jorge_20190812_184815_BURST002.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7c/Igreja_Matriz_de_S%C3%A3o_Jorge_20190812_184815_BURST002.jpg/220px-Igreja_Matriz_de_S%C3%A3o_Jorge_20190812_184815_BURST002.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7c/Igreja_Matriz_de_S%C3%A3o_Jorge_20190812_184815_BURST002.jpg/330px-Igreja_Matriz_de_S%C3%A3o_Jorge_20190812_184815_BURST002.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7c/Igreja_Matriz_de_S%C3%A3o_Jorge_20190812_184815_BURST002.jpg/440px-Igreja_Matriz_de_S%C3%A3o_Jorge_20190812_184815_BURST002.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4608" data-file-height="3456" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Relic" title="Relic">Relics</a> of George at <a href="/w/index.php?title=S%C3%A3o_Jorge_parish_church&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="São Jorge parish church (page does not exist)">São Jorge parish church</a>, <a href="/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Jorge_(Santana)" title="São Jorge (Santana)">São Jorge</a>, <a href="/wiki/Madeira_Island" title="Madeira Island">Madeira Island</a>, <a href="/wiki/Portugal" title="Portugal">Portugal</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>In the wake of the Crusades, George became a model of <a href="/wiki/Chivalry" title="Chivalry">chivalry</a> in works of literature, including <a href="/wiki/Medieval_romance" class="mw-redirect" title="Medieval romance">medieval romances</a>. In the 13th century, <a href="/wiki/Jacobus_de_Voragine" title="Jacobus de Voragine">Jacobus de Voragine</a>, Archbishop of Genoa, compiled the <i>Legenda Sanctorum</i>, (<i>Readings of the Saints</i>) also known as <i>Legenda Aurea</i> (the <i>Golden Legend</i>). Its 177 chapters (182 in some editions) include the story of George, among many others. After the invention of the printing press, the book became a best seller.
</p><p>The establishment of George as a popular saint and protective giant<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> in the West, that had captured the medieval imagination, was codified by the official elevation of his feast to a <i>festum duplex</i><sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> at a church council in 1415, on the date that had become associated with his martyrdom, 23 April. There was wide latitude from community to community in celebration of the day across late medieval and early modern England,<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> and no uniform "national" celebration elsewhere, a token of the popular and vernacular nature of George's <i>cultus</i> and its local horizons, supported by a local guild or confraternity under George's protection, or the dedication of a local church. When the <a href="/wiki/English_Reformation" title="English Reformation">English Reformation</a> severely curtailed the saints' days in the calendar, Saint George's Day was among the holidays that continued to be observed.
</p><p>In April 2019, the parish church of São Jorge, in <a href="/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Jorge_(Santana)" title="São Jorge (Santana)">São Jorge</a>, <a href="/wiki/Madeira_Island" title="Madeira Island">Madeira Island</a>, <a href="/wiki/Portugal" title="Portugal">Portugal</a>, solemnly received the <a href="/wiki/Relic" title="Relic">relics</a> of George, patron saint of the parish. During the celebrations the 504th anniversary of its foundation. the relics were brought by the new Bishop of Funchal, D. <a href="/wiki/Nuno_Br%C3%A1s_da_Silva_Martins" class="mw-redirect" title="Nuno Brás da Silva Martins">Nuno Brás</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Veneration_in_the_Levant">Veneration in the Levant</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Veneration in the Levant">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>George is renowned throughout the <a href="/wiki/Middle_East" title="Middle East">Middle East</a>, as both saint and prophet. His veneration by Christians and Muslims lies in his composite personality combining several biblical, Quranic and other ancient mythical heroes.<sup id="cite_ref-Hovannisian_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hovannisian-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> Saint George is the <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saint</a> of <a href="/wiki/Lebanese_Christians" class="mw-redirect" title="Lebanese Christians">Lebanese Christians</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Palestinian_Christians" title="Palestinian Christians">Palestinian Christians</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Christianity_in_Syria" title="Christianity in Syria">Syrian Christians</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:StGeorgeDragged.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f6/StGeorgeDragged.jpg/220px-StGeorgeDragged.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="466" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f6/StGeorgeDragged.jpg/330px-StGeorgeDragged.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f6/StGeorgeDragged.jpg/440px-StGeorgeDragged.jpg 2x" data-file-width="471" data-file-height="998" /></a><figcaption>Saint George dragged through the streets (detail), by <a href="/wiki/Bernat_Martorell" title="Bernat Martorell">Bernat Martorell</a>, 15th century</figcaption></figure>
<p><a href="/wiki/William_Dalrymple_(historian)" title="William Dalrymple (historian)">William Dalrymple</a>, who reviewed the literature in 1999, tells us that <a href="/wiki/J._E._Hanauer" title="J. E. Hanauer">J. E. Hanauer</a> in his 1907 book <i>Folklore of the Holy Land: Muslim, Christian and Jewish</i> "mentioned a shrine in the village of <a href="/wiki/Beit_Jala" title="Beit Jala">Beit Jala</a>, beside <a href="/wiki/Bethlehem" title="Bethlehem">Bethlehem</a>, which at the time was frequented by <a href="/wiki/Christians" title="Christians">Christians</a> who regarded it as the birthplace of George and some <a href="/wiki/Jews" title="Jews">Jews</a> who regarded it as the burial place of the <a href="/wiki/Elijah" title="Elijah">Prophet Elias</a>. According to Hanauer, in his day the monastery was "a sort of madhouse. Deranged persons of all the three faiths are taken thither and chained in the court of the chapel, where they are kept for forty days on bread and water, the Eastern Orthodox priest at the head of the establishment now and then reading the Gospel over them, or administering a whipping as the case demands."<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> In the 1920s, according to <a href="/wiki/Tawfiq_Canaan" title="Tawfiq Canaan">Tawfiq Canaan</a>'s <i>Mohammedan Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine</i>, nothing seemed to have changed, and all three communities were still visiting the shrine and praying together."<sup id="cite_ref-Dalrymple_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dalrymple-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Dalrymple himself visited the place in 1995. "I asked around in the <a href="/wiki/Christian_Quarter" title="Christian Quarter">Christian Quarter</a> in <a href="/wiki/Jerusalem" title="Jerusalem">Jerusalem</a>, and discovered that the place was very much alive. With all the greatest shrines in the Christian world to choose from, it seemed that when the local Arab Christians had a problem – an illness, or something more complicated – they preferred to seek the intercession of George in his grubby little shrine at Beit Jala rather than praying at the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_the_Holy_Sepulchre" title="Church of the Holy Sepulchre">Church of the Holy Sepulchre</a> in Jerusalem or the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_the_Nativity" title="Church of the Nativity">Church of the Nativity</a> in <a href="/wiki/Bethlehem" title="Bethlehem">Bethlehem</a>."<sup id="cite_ref-Dalrymple_53-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dalrymple-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> He asked the priest at the shrine "Do you get many Muslims coming here?" The priest replied, "We get hundreds! Almost as many as the Christian pilgrims. Often, when I come in here, I find Muslims all over the floor, in the aisles, up and down."<sup id="cite_ref-Dalrymple_53-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dalrymple-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <i>Encyclopædia Britannica</i> quotes G. A. Smith in his <i>Historic Geography of the Holy Land,</i> p.&#160;164, saying: "The Mahommedans who usually identify St. George with the prophet Elijah, at Lydda confound his legend with one about Christ himself. Their name for Antichrist is Dajjal, and they have a tradition that Jesus will slay Antichrist by the gate of Lydda. The notion sprang from an ancient <a href="/wiki/Bas-relief" class="mw-redirect" title="Bas-relief">bas-relief</a> of George and the Dragon on the Lydda church. But Dajjal may be derived, by a very common confusion between <i>n</i> and <i>l</i>, from Dagon, whose name two neighbouring villages bear to this day, while one of the gates of Lydda used to be called the Gate of Dagon."<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Veneration_in_the_Muslim_world">Veneration in the Muslim world</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Veneration in the Muslim world">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>George is described as a prophetic figure in Islamic sources.<sup id="cite_ref-Littlefield_32-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Littlefield-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> George is venerated by some Christians and Muslims because of his composite personality combining several biblical, Quranic and other ancient mythical heroes.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2018)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> In some sources he is identified with <a href="/wiki/Elijah" title="Elijah">Elijah</a> or Mar Elis, George or Mar Jirjus and in others as <a href="/wiki/Al-Khidr" class="mw-redirect" title="Al-Khidr">al-Khidr</a>. The last epithet meaning the "green prophet", is common to Christian, Muslim, and Druze folk piety. Samuel Curtiss who visited an artificial cave dedicated to him where he is identified with Elijah, reports that childless Muslim women used to visit the shrine to pray for children. Per tradition, he was brought to his place of martyrdom in chains, thus priests of Church of St. George chain the sick especially the mentally ill to a chain for overnight or longer for healing. This is sought after by both Muslims and Christians.<sup id="cite_ref-Hovannisian_48-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hovannisian-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_Anne_Finn" title="Elizabeth Anne Finn">Elizabeth Anne Finn</a>'s <i>Home in the Holy land</i> (1866):<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>St George killed the dragon in this country; and the place is shown close to <a href="/wiki/Beirut" title="Beirut">Beyroot</a>. Many churches and convents are named after him. The church at Lydda is dedicated to George; so is a convent near <a href="/wiki/Bethlehem" title="Bethlehem">Bethlehem</a>, and another small one just opposite the Jaffa gate, and others beside. The Arabs believe that George can restore mad people to their senses, and to say a person has been sent to St. George's is equivalent to saying he has been sent to a madhouse. It is singular that the Moslem Arabs adopted this veneration for St George, and send their mad people to be cured by him, as well as the Christians, but they commonly call him <a href="/wiki/Khidr" title="Khidr">El Khudder</a> – The Green
– according to their favourite manner of using epithets instead of names. Why he should be called green, however, I cannot tell  – unless it is from the colour of his horse. Gray horses are called green in Arabic.</p></blockquote>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Coin_of_Kvirike_III.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/Coin_of_Kvirike_III.jpg/300px-Coin_of_Kvirike_III.jpg" decoding="async" width="300" height="158" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/Coin_of_Kvirike_III.jpg/450px-Coin_of_Kvirike_III.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/5/5c/Coin_of_Kvirike_III.jpg 2x" data-file-width="551" data-file-height="291" /></a><figcaption>The earliest numismatic depiction of St. George. Coin of <a href="/wiki/Kvirike_III" class="mw-redirect" title="Kvirike III">Kvirike III</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Georgia" title="Kingdom of Georgia">Kingdom of Georgia</a>, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1015</span></figcaption></figure>
<p>The mosque of Nabi Jurjis, which was restored by <a href="/wiki/Timur" title="Timur">Timur</a> in the 14th century, was located in Mosul and supposedly contained the tomb of George.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> It was however destroyed in July 2014 by the <a href="/wiki/Islamic_State_of_Iraq_and_the_Levant" class="mw-redirect" title="Islamic State of Iraq and the Levant">Islamic State of Iraq and the Levant</a>, who also destroyed the Mosque of the Prophet Sheeth (<a href="/wiki/Seth" title="Seth">Seth</a>) and the Mosque of the Prophet Younis (<a href="/wiki/Jonah" title="Jonah">Jonah</a>). The militants claim such mosques have become places for apostasy instead of prayer.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>George or <i><a href="/wiki/Hazrat" class="mw-redirect" title="Hazrat">Hazrat</a></i> Jurjays was the patron saint of Mosul. Along with <a href="/wiki/Theodosius_the_Cenobiarch" title="Theodosius the Cenobiarch">Theodosius</a>, he was revered by both Christian and Muslim communities of <a href="/wiki/Jazira_Region" title="Jazira Region">Jazira</a> and <a href="/wiki/Anatolia" title="Anatolia">Anatolia</a>. The wall paintings of Kırk Dam Altı Kilise at <a href="/wiki/Belis%C4%B1rma" title="Belisırma">Belisırma</a> dedicated to him are dated between 1282 and 1304. These paintings depict him as a mounted knight appearing between donors including a Georgian lady called Thamar and her husband, the Emir and Consul Basil, while the Seljuk Sultan <a href="/wiki/Mesud_II" title="Mesud II">Mesud II</a> and Byzantine Emperor <a href="/wiki/Andronikos_II_Palaiologos" title="Andronikos II Palaiologos">Androncius II</a> are also named in the inscriptions.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A <a href="/wiki/Maqam_(shrine)" title="Maqam (shrine)">shrine</a> attributed to prophet George can be found in <a href="/wiki/Diyarbakir" class="mw-redirect" title="Diyarbakir">Diyarbakir</a>, Turkey. <a href="/wiki/Evliya_%C3%87elebi" title="Evliya Çelebi">Evliya Çelebi</a> states in his <i><a href="/wiki/Seyahatname" class="mw-redirect" title="Seyahatname">Seyahatname</a></i> that he visited the tombs of prophet <a href="/wiki/Jonah" title="Jonah">Jonah</a> and prophet George in the city.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Feast_days">Feast days</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Feast days">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Saint_George_in_devotions,_traditions_and_prayers" title="Saint George in devotions, traditions and prayers">Saint George in devotions, traditions and prayers</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Dante_Gabriel_Rossetti_-_The_Wedding_of_St_George_and_Princess_Sabra.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Dante_Gabriel_Rossetti_-_The_Wedding_of_St_George_and_Princess_Sabra.jpg/300px-Dante_Gabriel_Rossetti_-_The_Wedding_of_St_George_and_Princess_Sabra.jpg" decoding="async" width="300" height="302" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Dante_Gabriel_Rossetti_-_The_Wedding_of_St_George_and_Princess_Sabra.jpg/450px-Dante_Gabriel_Rossetti_-_The_Wedding_of_St_George_and_Princess_Sabra.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Dante_Gabriel_Rossetti_-_The_Wedding_of_St_George_and_Princess_Sabra.jpg/600px-Dante_Gabriel_Rossetti_-_The_Wedding_of_St_George_and_Princess_Sabra.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1525" data-file-height="1536" /></a><figcaption><i>The Wedding of St George and Princess Sabra</i> by <a href="/wiki/Dante_Gabriel_Rossetti" title="Dante Gabriel Rossetti">Dante Gabriel Rossetti</a> (1857)</figcaption></figure>
<p>In the <a href="/wiki/General_Roman_Calendar" title="General Roman Calendar">General Roman Calendar</a>, the feast of George is on 23 April. In the <a href="/wiki/Tridentine_Calendar" class="mw-redirect" title="Tridentine Calendar">Tridentine Calendar</a> of 1568, it was given the <a href="/wiki/Ranking_of_liturgical_days_in_the_Roman_Rite" title="Ranking of liturgical days in the Roman Rite">rank</a> of "Semidouble". In <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XII" title="Pope Pius XII">Pope Pius XII</a>'s <a href="/wiki/General_Roman_Calendar_of_Pope_Pius_XII" title="General Roman Calendar of Pope Pius XII">1955 calendar</a> this rank was reduced to "Simple", and in <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_XXIII" title="Pope John XXIII">Pope John XXIII</a>'s <a href="/wiki/General_Roman_Calendar_of_1960" title="General Roman Calendar of 1960">1960 calendar</a> to a <a href="/wiki/Commemoration_(liturgy)" title="Commemoration (liturgy)">"Commemoration"</a>. Since <a href="/wiki/Pope_Paul_VI" title="Pope Paul VI">Pope Paul VI</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Mysterii_Paschalis" title="Mysterii Paschalis">1969 revision</a>, it appears as an <a href="/wiki/Memorial_(liturgy)" title="Memorial (liturgy)">"optional memorial</a>". In some countries such as <a href="/wiki/England" title="England">England</a>, the rank is higher – it is a Solemnity (Roman Catholic) or Feast (<a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a>): if it falls between Palm Sunday and the Second Sunday of Easter inclusive, it is transferred to the Monday after the Second Sunday of Easter.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>George is very much honoured by the Eastern Orthodox Church, wherein he is referred to as a "Great Martyr", and in <a href="/wiki/Oriental_Orthodoxy" class="mw-redirect" title="Oriental Orthodoxy">Oriental Orthodoxy</a> overall. His <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints" title="Calendar of saints">major feast day</a> is on 23 April (Julian calendar 23 April currently corresponds to Gregorian calendar 6 May). If, however, the feast occurs before <a href="/wiki/Easter" title="Easter">Easter</a>, it is celebrated on <a href="/wiki/Easter_Monday" title="Easter Monday">Easter Monday</a>, instead. The Russian Orthodox Church also celebrates two additional feasts in honour of George. One is on 3 November, commemorating the <a href="/wiki/Consecration" class="mw-redirect" title="Consecration">consecration</a> of a <a href="/wiki/Cathedral" title="Cathedral">cathedral</a> dedicated to him in Lydda during the reign of <a href="/wiki/Constantine_the_Great" title="Constantine the Great">Constantine the Great</a> (305–37). When the church was consecrated, the <a href="/wiki/Relic" title="Relic">relics</a> of George were transferred there. The other feast is on 26 November for a church dedicated to him in <a href="/wiki/Kiev" class="mw-redirect" title="Kiev">Kiev</a>, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1054</span>.
</p><p>In <a href="/wiki/Bulgaria" title="Bulgaria">Bulgaria</a>, George's day (<a href="/wiki/Bulgarian_language" title="Bulgarian language">Bulgarian</a>: <span lang="bg">Гергьовден</span>) is celebrated on 6 May, when it is customary to slaughter and roast a lamb. George's day is also a <a href="/wiki/Public_holiday" title="Public holiday">public holiday</a>.
</p><p>In <a href="/wiki/Serbia" title="Serbia">Serbia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bosnia_and_Herzegovina" title="Bosnia and Herzegovina">Bosnia and Herzegovina</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Serbian_Orthodox_Church" title="Serbian Orthodox Church">Serbian Orthodox Church</a> refers to George as <i>Sveti Djordje</i> (<i>Свети Ђорђе</i>) or <i>Sveti Georgije</i> (<i>Свети Георгије</i>). George's day (<i>Đurđevdan</i>) is celebrated on 6 May, and is a common <a href="/wiki/Slava_(patron_saint_day)" class="mw-redirect" title="Slava (patron saint day)"><i>slava</i> (patron saint day)</a> among ethnic <a href="/wiki/Serbs" title="Serbs">Serbs</a>.
</p><p>In <a href="/wiki/Egypt" title="Egypt">Egypt</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Coptic_Orthodox_Church_of_Alexandria" class="mw-redirect" title="Coptic Orthodox Church of Alexandria">Coptic Orthodox Church of Alexandria</a> refers to George (<a href="/wiki/Coptic_language" title="Coptic language">Coptic</a>: <span lang="cop">Ⲡⲓⲇⲅⲓⲟⲥ Ⲅⲉⲟⲣⲅⲓⲟⲥ or ⲅⲉⲱⲣⲅⲓⲟⲥ</span>) as the "Prince of Martyrs" and celebrates his martyrdom on the 23rd of <a href="/wiki/Paremhat" title="Paremhat">Paremhat</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Coptic_calendar" title="Coptic calendar">Coptic calendar</a>, equivalent to 1 May.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Copts" title="Copts">Copts</a> also celebrate the consecration of the first church dedicated to him on the seventh of the month of Hatour of the Coptic calendar usually equivalent to 17 November.
</p><p>In <a href="/wiki/India" title="India">India</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Syro-Malabar_Catholic_Church" title="Syro-Malabar Catholic Church">Syro-Malabar Catholic Church</a>, one of the oriental catholic churches (<a href="/wiki/Eastern_Catholic_Churches" title="Eastern Catholic Churches">Eastern Catholic Churches</a>), and Malankara Orthodox Church venerate George. The main pilgrim centres of the saint in India are at Aruvithura and Puthuppally in Kottayam District, <a href="/wiki/Edathua" title="Edathua">Edathua</a><sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> in <a href="/wiki/Alappuzha" title="Alappuzha">Alappuzha</a> district, and <a href="/wiki/Edappally" title="Edappally">Edappally</a><sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> in <a href="/wiki/Ernakulam" title="Ernakulam">Ernakulam</a> district of the southern state of <a href="/wiki/Kerala" title="Kerala">Kerala</a>. The saint is commemorated each year from 27 April to 14 May at Edathua.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> On 27 April after the flag hoisting ceremony by the parish priest, the statue of the saint is taken from one of the altars and placed at the extension of the church to be venerated by devotees till 14 May. The main feast day is 7 May, when the statue of the saint along with other saints is taken in procession around the church. Intercession to George of Edathua is believed to be efficacious in repelling snakes and in curing mental ailments. The sacred relics of George were brought to <a href="/wiki/Antioch" title="Antioch">Antioch</a> from <a href="/wiki/Mardin" title="Mardin">Mardin</a> in 900 and were taken to Kerala, India, from Antioch in 1912 by Mar Dionysius of Vattasseril and kept in the Orthodox seminary at Kundara, Kerala. H.H. Mathews II Catholicos had given the relics to St. George churches at Puthupally, Kottayam District, and Chandanappally, Pathanamthitta district.
</p><p>George is <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">remembered</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a> with a <a href="/wiki/Festival_(Anglicanism)" title="Festival (Anglicanism)">Festival</a> on 23 April.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Catholic Church feast days:
</p>
<ul><li>23 April – main commemoration,<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>24 April – commemoration in <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Poland" title="Catholic Church in Poland">Poland</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> (23 April – commemoration of <a href="/wiki/Saint_Wojciech" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Wojciech">Saint Wojciech</a>)<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>7 May – <a href="/wiki/Martyrdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Martyrdom">martyrdom</a> in <a href="/wiki/Lydda" class="mw-redirect" title="Lydda">Lydda</a>,</li>
<li>20 June – commemoration of <a href="/wiki/Translation_(relic)" title="Translation (relic)">translation</a> of <a href="/wiki/Sacred_Relic_of_Saint_George" title="Sacred Relic of Saint George">relics</a> to <a href="/wiki/Anchin_Abbey" title="Anchin Abbey">Anchin Abbey</a>,</li>
<li>15 October – commemoration of <a href="/wiki/Translation_(relic)" title="Translation (relic)">translation</a> of <a href="/wiki/Sacred_Relic_of_Saint_George" title="Sacred Relic of Saint George">relics</a> to <a href="/wiki/Toulouse" title="Toulouse">Toulouse</a>,<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (March 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup></li></ul>
<p>Eastern Orthodox Church feast days:<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul><li>27 January – Commemoration of the Miracle (deliverance of the island of <a href="/wiki/Zakynthos" title="Zakynthos">Zakynthos</a> from the plague) of the Great Martyr George in <a href="/wiki/Zakynthos" title="Zakynthos">Zakynthos</a> in 1689/1688. (<a href="/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Church" title="Greek Orthodox Church">Greek Orthodox Church</a>)<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>12 April – Gerontius from Cappadocia, <a href="/wiki/Martyr" title="Martyr">martyr</a>, father of George, husband of Polychronia (c.290)<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>23 April – Holy Glorious <a href="/wiki/Great_martyr" title="Great martyr">Great-martyr</a>, Victory-bearer and <a href="/wiki/Wonderworker" class="mw-redirect" title="Wonderworker">Wonderworker</a> George (303) [<a href="/wiki/Death_anniversary" title="Death anniversary">Death anniversary</a>]</li>
<li>23 April – Polychronia from Cappadocia, martyr, mother of George, wife of Gerontius (303/304)<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>6 May – <a href="/wiki/George%27s_Day_in_Spring" title="George&#39;s Day in Spring">George's Day in Spring</a> [<a href="/wiki/Bulgarian_Orthodox_Church" title="Bulgarian Orthodox Church">BOC</a>]</li>
<li>3 November – Dedication of the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_Saint_George_and_Mosque_of_Al-Khadr" class="mw-redirect" title="Church of Saint George and Mosque of Al-Khadr">Church of the Great-martyr George</a> in <a href="/wiki/Lydda" class="mw-redirect" title="Lydda">Lydda</a> (4th century)</li>
<li>10 November – Commemoration of the torture of Great-martyr George in 303 [<a href="/wiki/Georgian_Orthodox_Church" title="Georgian Orthodox Church">GOC</a>]<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>23 November – Dedication of the <a href="/wiki/Saint_George%27s_Day" title="Saint George&#39;s Day">Church of St. George</a> at <a href="/wiki/Kiev" class="mw-redirect" title="Kiev">Kiev</a> (1051)</li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Patronages">Patronages</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Patronages">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Patronages_of_Saint_George" title="Patronages of Saint George">Patronages of Saint George</a></div>
<p>George is a highly celebrated saint in both the <a href="/wiki/Catholic" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholic">Western</a> and <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Christian" class="mw-redirect" title="Eastern Christian">Eastern Christian</a> churches, and many <a href="/wiki/Patronages_of_Saint_George" title="Patronages of Saint George">Patronages of Saint George</a> exist throughout the world.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>George is the <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saint</a> of <a href="/wiki/England" title="England">England</a>. His cross forms the national <a href="/wiki/Flag_of_England" title="Flag of England">flag of England</a>, which also (through the structure of <a href="/wiki/England_and_Wales" title="England and Wales">England and Wales</a>) represents <a href="/wiki/Wales" title="Wales">Wales</a> within the <a href="/wiki/Union_Flag" class="mw-redirect" title="Union Flag">Union Flag</a> of the <a href="/wiki/United_Kingdom" title="United Kingdom">United Kingdom</a> and other national flags containing the Union Flag, such as those of <a href="/wiki/Australia" title="Australia">Australia</a> and <a href="/wiki/New_Zealand" title="New Zealand">New Zealand</a>. By the 14th century, the saint had been declared both the patron saint and the protector of the royal family.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>George has been the patron saint of <a href="/wiki/Bosnia_and_Herzegovina" title="Bosnia and Herzegovina">Bosnia and Herzegovina</a> from the <a href="/wiki/Bosnia_and_Herzegovina_in_the_Middle_Ages" title="Bosnia and Herzegovina in the Middle Ages">medieval times</a> until 26 August 1752, when he was replaced by <a href="/wiki/Elijah" title="Elijah">Elijah</a> at the request of a <a href="/wiki/Bosnian_Franciscan" class="mw-redirect" title="Bosnian Franciscan">Bosnian Franciscan</a> friar, Bishop <a href="/wiki/Pavao_Dragi%C4%8Devi%C4%87" title="Pavao Dragičević">Pavao Dragičević</a>. The reasons for the replacement are unclear. It has been suggested that Elijah was chosen because of his importance to all three main religious groups in Bosnia and Herzegovina—<a href="/wiki/Catholicism_in_Bosnia_and_Herzegovina" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholicism in Bosnia and Herzegovina">Catholics</a>, <a href="/wiki/Islam_in_Bosnia_and_Herzegovina" title="Islam in Bosnia and Herzegovina">Muslims</a> and <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodoxy_in_Bosnia_and_Herzegovina" title="Eastern Orthodoxy in Bosnia and Herzegovina">Orthodox Christians</a>. <a href="/wiki/Pope_Benedict_XIV" title="Pope Benedict XIV">Pope Benedict XIV</a> is said to have approved Bishop Dragičević's request with the remark that "a wild nation deserved a wild patron".<sup id="cite_ref-Skoko_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Skoko-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Martić_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martić-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>George is the patron saint of <a href="/wiki/Ethiopia" title="Ethiopia">Ethiopia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> He is also the patron saint of the <a href="/wiki/Ethiopian_Orthodox_Tewahedo_Church" title="Ethiopian Orthodox Tewahedo Church">Ethiopian Orthodox Church</a>; George slaying the dragon is one of the most frequently used subjects of <a href="/wiki/Icon" title="Icon">icons</a> in the church.<b><sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup></b>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:2014_Tbilisi,_Pomnik_Wolno%C5%9Bci_z_konnym_pos%C4%85giem_%C5%9Bwi%C4%99tego_Jerzego_(07).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/2014_Tbilisi%2C_Pomnik_Wolno%C5%9Bci_z_konnym_pos%C4%85giem_%C5%9Bwi%C4%99tego_Jerzego_%2807%29.jpg/220px-2014_Tbilisi%2C_Pomnik_Wolno%C5%9Bci_z_konnym_pos%C4%85giem_%C5%9Bwi%C4%99tego_Jerzego_%2807%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="144" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/2014_Tbilisi%2C_Pomnik_Wolno%C5%9Bci_z_konnym_pos%C4%85giem_%C5%9Bwi%C4%99tego_Jerzego_%2807%29.jpg/330px-2014_Tbilisi%2C_Pomnik_Wolno%C5%9Bci_z_konnym_pos%C4%85giem_%C5%9Bwi%C4%99tego_Jerzego_%2807%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/2014_Tbilisi%2C_Pomnik_Wolno%C5%9Bci_z_konnym_pos%C4%85giem_%C5%9Bwi%C4%99tego_Jerzego_%2807%29.jpg/440px-2014_Tbilisi%2C_Pomnik_Wolno%C5%9Bci_z_konnym_pos%C4%85giem_%C5%9Bwi%C4%99tego_Jerzego_%2807%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4674" data-file-height="3061" /></a><figcaption>Monument dedicated to St George in the Georgian capital of <a href="/wiki/Tbilisi" title="Tbilisi">Tbilisi</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Georgia_(country)" title="Georgia (country)">country of Georgia</a>, where devotions to the saint date back to the fourth century, is not technically named after the saint, but is a well-attested <a href="/wiki/Back-formation" title="Back-formation">back-formation</a> of the English name. However, many towns and cities around the world are. George is one of the patron saints of Georgia. Exactly 365 Orthodox churches in Georgia are named after George according to the number of days in a year. According to legend, George was cut into 365 pieces after he fell in battle and every single piece was spread throughout the entire country.<sup id="cite_ref-Gabidzashvili_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gabidzashvili-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>George is also one of the patron saints of the Mediterranean islands of <a href="/wiki/Malta" title="Malta">Malta</a> and <a href="/wiki/Gozo" title="Gozo">Gozo</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> In a battle between the Maltese and the <a href="/wiki/Moors" title="Moors">Moors</a>, George was alleged to have been seen with <a href="/wiki/Saint_Paul" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Paul">Saint Paul</a> and <a href="/wiki/Saint_Agata" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Agata">Saint Agata</a>, protecting the Maltese. George is the protector of the island of Gozo and the patron of Gozo's largest city, <a href="/wiki/Victoria,_Gozo" title="Victoria, Gozo">Victoria</a>. The <a href="/wiki/St._George%27s_Basilica,_Malta" title="St. George&#39;s Basilica, Malta">St. George's Basilica</a> in Victoria is dedicated to him.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Britain_Needs_You_at_Once_-_WWI_recruitment_poster_-_Parliamentary_Recruiting_Committee_Poster_No._108.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Britain_Needs_You_at_Once_-_WWI_recruitment_poster_-_Parliamentary_Recruiting_Committee_Poster_No._108.jpg/180px-Britain_Needs_You_at_Once_-_WWI_recruitment_poster_-_Parliamentary_Recruiting_Committee_Poster_No._108.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="274" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Britain_Needs_You_at_Once_-_WWI_recruitment_poster_-_Parliamentary_Recruiting_Committee_Poster_No._108.jpg/270px-Britain_Needs_You_at_Once_-_WWI_recruitment_poster_-_Parliamentary_Recruiting_Committee_Poster_No._108.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Britain_Needs_You_at_Once_-_WWI_recruitment_poster_-_Parliamentary_Recruiting_Committee_Poster_No._108.jpg/360px-Britain_Needs_You_at_Once_-_WWI_recruitment_poster_-_Parliamentary_Recruiting_Committee_Poster_No._108.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2534" data-file-height="3852" /></a><figcaption>English recruitment poster from <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a>, featuring George and the Dragon</figcaption></figure>
<p>Devotions to George in <a href="/wiki/Portugal" title="Portugal">Portugal</a> date back to the 12th century. <a href="/wiki/Nuno_%C3%81lvares_Pereira" title="Nuno Álvares Pereira">Nuno Álvares Pereira</a> attributed the victory of the Portuguese in the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Aljubarrota" title="Battle of Aljubarrota">battle of Aljubarrota</a> in 1385 to George. During the reign of <a href="/wiki/John_I_of_Portugal" title="John I of Portugal">John I of Portugal</a> (1357–1433), George became the patron saint of Portugal and the King ordered that the saint's image on the horse be carried in the <i><a href="/wiki/Corpus_Christi_(feast)" class="mw-redirect" title="Corpus Christi (feast)">Corpus Christi</a></i> procession. The flag of George (white with red cross) was also carried by the Portuguese troops and hoisted in the fortresses, during the 15th century. "Portugal and Saint George" became the battle cry of the Portuguese troops, being still today the battle cry of the <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_Army" title="Portuguese Army">Portuguese Army</a>, with simply "Saint George" being the battle cry of the <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_Navy" title="Portuguese Navy">Portuguese Navy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Devotions to Saint George in <a href="/wiki/Brazil" title="Brazil">Brazil</a> was influenced by the Portuguese colonisation. George is the unofficial patron saint of the city of <a href="/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro" title="Rio de Janeiro">Rio de Janeiro</a> (title officially attributed to <a href="/wiki/Saint_Sebastian" title="Saint Sebastian">Saint Sebastian</a>) and of the city of São Jorge dos Ilhéus (Saint George of Ilhéus). Additionally, George is the patron saint of Scouts and of the Cavalry of the Brazilian Army. In May 2019, he was made official as the patron saint of the State of Rio de Janeiro, next to Saint Sebastian.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> George is also revered in several Afro-Brazilian religions, such as <a href="/wiki/Umbanda" title="Umbanda">Umbanda</a>, where it is syncretized in the form of Ogum. However, the connection of George with the Moon is purely Brazilian, with a strong influence of African culture, and in no way related to the European saint. Tradition says that the spots at the Moon's surface represent the miraculous saint, his horse and his sword slaying the dragon and ready to defend those who seek his help.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>George, is also the patron saint of the region of <a href="/wiki/Aragon" title="Aragon">Aragon</a>, in <a href="/wiki/Spain" title="Spain">Spain</a>, where his feast day is celebrated on 23 April and is known as "Aragon Day", <i>or 'Día de Aragón'</i> in Spanish.
