He became the patron saint of the former <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Aragon" title="Kingdom of Aragon">Kingdom of Aragon</a> and <a href="/wiki/Crown_of_Aragon" title="Crown of Aragon">Crown of Aragon</a> when King <a href="/wiki/Pedro_I_of_Aragon" class="mw-redirect" title="Pedro I of Aragon">Pedro I of Aragon</a> won the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Alcoraz" title="Battle of Alcoraz">Battle of Alcoraz</a> in 1096. Legend has it that victory eventually fell to the Christian armies when George appeared to them on the battlefield, helping them secure the conquest of the city of <a href="/wiki/Huesca" title="Huesca">Huesca</a> which had been under the Muslim control of the <a href="/wiki/Taifa" title="Taifa">Taifa</a> of <a href="/wiki/Saragossa" class="mw-redirect" title="Saragossa">Zaragoza</a>. The battle, which had begun two years earlier in 1094, was long and arduous, and had also taken the life of King Pedro's own father, King <a href="/wiki/Sancho_Ramirez" class="mw-redirect" title="Sancho Ramirez">Sancho Ramirez</a>. With the Aragonese spirits flagging, it is said that George descending from heaven on his charger and bearing a dark red cross, appeared at the head of the Christian cavalry leading the knights into battle. Interpreting this as a sign of protection from God, the Christian militia returned emboldened to the battle field, more energised than ever, convinced theirs was the banner of the one true faith. Defeated, the <a href="/wiki/Moors" title="Moors">moors</a> rapidly abandoned the battlefield. After two years of being locked down under siege, Huesca fell and King Pedro made his triumphal entry into the city. To celebrate this victory, the cross of St. George was adopted as the personal coat of arms of Huesca and Aragon, in honour of their saviour. After the fall of Huesca, King Pedro aided the military leader and nobleman, Rodrigo Díaz de Vivar, otherwise known as <a href="/wiki/El_Cid" title="El Cid">El Cid</a>, with a coalition army from Aragon in the long conquest of the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Valencia" title="Kingdom of Valencia">Kingdom of Valencia</a>.
</p><p>Tales of King Pedro's success at Huesca and in leading his expedition of armies with El Cid against the Moors, under the auspices of George on his standard, spread quickly throughout the realm and beyond the Crown of Aragon, and Christian armies throughout Europe quickly began adopting George as their protector and patron, during all subsequent Crusades to the Holy Lands. By 1117, the military order of Templars adopted the Cross of St. George as a simple, unifying sign for international Christian militia embroidered on the left hand side of their tunics, placed above the heart.
</p><p>The Cross of St. George, also known in Aragon as The <a href="/wiki/Cross_of_Alcoraz" title="Cross of Alcoraz">Cross of Alcoraz</a>, continues to emblazon the flags of all of Aragon's provinces.
</p><p>The association of St. George with chivalry and noblemen in Aragon continued through the ages. Indeed, even the author <a href="/wiki/Miguel_de_Cervantes" title="Miguel de Cervantes">Miguel de Cervantes</a>, in his book on the adventures of <a href="/wiki/Don_Quixote" title="Don Quixote">Don Quixote</a>, also mentions the <a href="/wiki/Jousting" title="Jousting">jousting</a> events that took place at the festival of St. George in <a href="/wiki/Saragossa" class="mw-redirect" title="Saragossa">Zaragoza</a> in Aragon where one could gain international renown in winning a joust against any of the knights of Aragon.
</p><p>In Valencia, Catalonia, the Balearics, Malta, Sicily and Sardinia, the origins of the veneration of St. George go back to their shared history as territories under the <a href="/wiki/Crown_of_Aragon" title="Crown of Aragon">Crown of Aragon</a>, thereby sharing the same legend.
</p><p>One of the highest civil distinctions awarded in Catalonia is the <a href="/wiki/Creu_de_Sant_Jordi_Award" class="mw-redirect" title="Creu de Sant Jordi Award">St. George's Cross</a> (<i>Creu de Sant Jordi</i>). The <a href="/wiki/Sant_Jordi_Awards" title="Sant Jordi Awards">Sant Jordi Awards</a> have been awarded in Barcelona since 1957.
</p><p>Saint George (<i>Sant Jordi</i> in <a href="/wiki/Catalan_language" title="Catalan language">Catalan</a>) is also the patron saint of <a href="/wiki/Catalonia" title="Catalonia">Catalonia</a>. His cross appears in many buildings and local flags, including the <a href="/wiki/Flag_of_Barcelona" title="Flag of Barcelona">flag of Barcelona</a>, the Catalan capital. A Catalan variation to the traditional legend places George's life story as having occurred in the town of <a href="/wiki/Montblanc,_Tarragona" title="Montblanc, Tarragona">Montblanc</a>, near <a href="/wiki/Tarragona" title="Tarragona">Tarragona</a>.
</p><p>In 1469, the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_St._George_(Habsburg-Lorraine)" title="Order of St. George (Habsburg-Lorraine)">Order of St. George (Habsburg-Lorraine)</a> was founded in Rome by <a href="/wiki/Frederick_III,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Frederick III, Holy Roman Emperor">Emperor Friedrich III of Habsburg</a> in the presence of <a href="/wiki/Pope_Paul_II" title="Pope Paul II">Pope Paul II</a> in honour of Saint George. The order was continued and promoted by his son, <a href="/wiki/Maximilian_I,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Maximilian I, Holy Roman Emperor">Emperor Maximilian of Habsburg</a>. The later history of the order was eventful, in particular the order was dissolved by Nazi Germany. Only after the fall of the Iron Curtain and the collapse of communism in Central and Eastern Europe was the order reactivated as a European association in association with Saint George by the <a href="/wiki/Habsburg" class="mw-redirect" title="Habsburg">Habsburg</a> family.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Arms_and_flag">Arms and flag</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Arms and flag">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Saint_George%27s_Cross" title="Saint George&#39;s Cross">Saint George's Cross</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Flag_of_England.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/be/Flag_of_England.svg/170px-Flag_of_England.svg.png" decoding="async" width="170" height="102" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/be/Flag_of_England.svg/255px-Flag_of_England.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/be/Flag_of_England.svg/340px-Flag_of_England.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="480" /></a><figcaption>George's cross</figcaption></figure>
<p>It became fashionable in the 15th century, with the full development of classical heraldry, to provide <a href="/wiki/Attributed_arms" title="Attributed arms">attributed arms</a> to saints and other historical characters from the pre-heraldic ages. The widespread attribution to George of the red cross on a white field in Western art – "<a href="/wiki/Saint_George%27s_Cross" title="Saint George&#39;s Cross">Saint George's Cross</a>" – probably first arose in <a href="/wiki/Genoa" title="Genoa">Genoa</a>, which had adopted this image for <a href="/wiki/Genoa#Flag" title="Genoa">their flag</a> and George as their patron saint in the 12th century. A <i>vexillum beati Georgii</i> is mentioned in the Genovese annals for the year 1198, referring to a red flag with a depiction of George and the dragon. An illumination of this flag is shown in the annals for the year 1227. The Genovese flag with the red cross was used alongside this "George's flag", from at least 1218, and was known as the <i>insignia cruxata comunis Janue</i> ("cross ensign of the commune of Genoa"). The flag showing the saint himself was the city's principal war flag, but the flag showing the plain cross was used alongside it in the 1240s.<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1348, <a href="/wiki/Edward_III_of_England" title="Edward III of England">Edward III of England</a> chose George as the patron saint of his <a href="/wiki/Order_of_the_Garter" title="Order of the Garter">Order of the Garter</a>, and also took to using a red-on-white cross in the hoist of his <a href="/wiki/Royal_Standards_of_England" class="mw-redirect" title="Royal Standards of England">Royal Standard</a>.
</p><p>The term "Saint George's cross" was at first associated with any plain <a href="/wiki/Christian_cross_variants#Greek_cross" title="Christian cross variants">Greek cross</a> touching the edges of the field (not necessarily red on white).<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Fuller" title="Thomas Fuller">Thomas Fuller</a> in 1647 spoke of "the plain or St George's cross" as "the mother of all the others" (that is, the other <a href="/wiki/Crosses_in_heraldry" title="Crosses in heraldry">heraldic crosses</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Iconography">Iconography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Iconography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Icon8.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cf/Icon8.jpg/170px-Icon8.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="258" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cf/Icon8.jpg/255px-Icon8.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cf/Icon8.jpg/340px-Icon8.jpg 2x" data-file-width="396" data-file-height="600" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Byzantine" class="mw-redirect" title="Byzantine">Byzantine</a> icon of George, <a href="/wiki/Athens" title="Athens">Athens</a>, Greece</figcaption></figure>
<p>George is most commonly depicted in early icons, <a href="/wiki/Mosaic" title="Mosaic">mosaics</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Fresco" title="Fresco">frescos</a> wearing armour contemporary with the depiction, executed in gilding and silver colour, intended to identify him as a <a href="/wiki/Roman_army" title="Roman army">Roman soldier</a>. Particularly after the <a href="/wiki/Fall_of_Constantinople" title="Fall of Constantinople">Fall of Constantinople</a> and George's association with the <a href="/wiki/Crusades" title="Crusades">crusades</a>, he is often portrayed mounted upon a <a href="/wiki/White_horse_(mythology)" class="mw-redirect" title="White horse (mythology)">white horse</a>. Thus, a 2003 Vatican stamp (issued on the anniversary of the Saint's death) depicts an armoured George atop a white horse, killing the dragon.<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Eastern Orthodox iconography also permits George to ride a black horse, as in a Russian icon in the British museum collection.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup>
In the south Lebanese village of <a href="/wiki/Mieh_Mieh" class="mw-redirect" title="Mieh Mieh">Mieh Mieh</a>, the Saint George Church for Melkite Catholics commissioned for its 75th jubilee in 2012 (under the guidance of Mgr Sassine Gregoire) the only icons in the world portraying the whole life of George, as well as the scenes of his torture and martyrdom (drawn in eastern iconographic style).<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>George may also be portrayed with <a href="/wiki/Saint_Demetrius" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Demetrius">Saint Demetrius</a>, another early <a href="/wiki/Soldier_saint" class="mw-redirect" title="Soldier saint">soldier saint</a>. When the two saintly warriors are together and mounted upon horses, they may resemble earthly manifestations of the archangels <a href="/wiki/Michael_(archangel)" title="Michael (archangel)">Michael</a> and <a href="/wiki/Gabriel" title="Gabriel">Gabriel</a>. Eastern traditions distinguish the two as George rides a white horse and Demetrius a red horse (the red <a href="/wiki/Pigment" title="Pigment">pigment</a> may appear black if it has bituminized). George can also be identified by his spearing a dragon, whereas Demetrius may be spearing a human figure, representing <a href="/wiki/Maximian" title="Maximian">Maximian</a>.
</p>

<li id="cite_note-9"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-9">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="/wiki/Church_History_(Eusebius)" title="Church History (Eusebius)">Church History (Eusebius)</a>, book 8, chapter 5; <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/text?doc=Perseus%3Atext%3A2008.01.0640%3Abook%3D8%3Achapter%3D5%3Asection%3D1">Greek text here</a> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://web.archive.org/web/20220114032922/https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/text?doc=Perseus:text:2008.01.0640:book%3D8:chapter%3D5:section%3D1">Archived</a> 14 January 2022 at the <a href="/wiki/Wayback_Machine" title="Wayback Machine">Wayback Machine</a>, and <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.documentacatholicaomnia.eu/03d/0265-0339,_Eusebius_Caesariensis,_Historia_ecclesiastica_%5BSchaff%5D,_EN.pdf">English text here</a>. Eusebius's full text as follows:
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Immediately on the publication of the <a href="/wiki/20,000_Martyrs_of_Nicomedia" title="20,000 Martyrs of Nicomedia">decree against the churches in Nicomedia</a>, a certain man, not obscure but very highly honored with distinguished temporal dignities, moved with zeal toward God, and incited with ardent faith, seized the edict as it was posted openly and publicly, and tore it to pieces as a profane and impious thing; and this was done while two of the sovereigns were in the same city,—the oldest of all, and the one who held the fourth place in the government after him. But this man, first in that place, after distinguishing himself in such a manner suffered those things which were likely to follow such daring, and kept his spirit cheerful and undisturbed till death.</p></blockquote></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-10"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-10">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFWalter2003" class="citation cs2">Walter, Christopher (2003), <i>The Warrior Saints in Byzantine Art and Tradition</i>, Ashgate Publishing, p.&#160;110, <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/1-84014-694-X" title="Special:BookSources/1-84014-694-X"><bdi>1-84014-694-X</bdi></a></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=The+Warrior+Saints+in+Byzantine+Art+and+Tradition&amp;rft.pages=110&amp;rft.pub=Ashgate+Publishing&amp;rft.date=2003&amp;rft.isbn=1-84014-694-X&amp;rft.aulast=Walter&amp;rft.aufirst=Christopher&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ASaint+George" class="Z3988"></span>.
Bibliotheca Hagiographica Graeca 271, 272.</span>
</li>

<li id="cite_note-20"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-20">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Michael Collins, <i>St George and the Dragons: The Making of English Identity</i> (2018), <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=Z95VDwAAQBAJ&amp;pg=PT129">p. 129</a> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://web.archive.org/web/20221113181917/https://books.google.com/books?id=Z95VDwAAQBAJ&amp;pg=PT129">Archived</a> 13 November 2022 at the <a href="/wiki/Wayback_Machine" title="Wayback Machine">Wayback Machine</a>.</span>

<li id="cite_note-CE1913-21"><span class="mw-cite-backlink">^ <a href="#cite_ref-CE1913_21-0"><sup><i><b>a</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-CE1913_21-1"><sup><i><b>b</b></i></sup></a></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFThurston1913" class="citation encyclopaedia cs1"><a href="/wiki/Herbert_Thurston" title="Herbert Thurston">Thurston, Herbert</a> (1913). <span class="cs1-ws-icon" title="s:Catholic Encyclopedia (1913)/St. George"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Catholic_Encyclopedia_(1913)/St._George">"St. George"&#160;</a></span>. In Herbermann, Charles (ed.). <i><a href="/wiki/Catholic_Encyclopedia" title="Catholic Encyclopedia">Catholic Encyclopedia</a></i>. New York: Robert Appleton Company.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=bookitem&amp;rft.atitle=St.+George&amp;rft.btitle=Catholic+Encyclopedia&amp;rft.place=New+York&amp;rft.pub=Robert+Appleton+Company&amp;rft.date=1913&amp;rft.aulast=Thurston&amp;rft.aufirst=Herbert&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ASaint+George" class="Z3988"></span> "There seems, therefore, no ground for doubting the historical existence of St. George, even though he is not commemorated in the Syrian, or in the primitive Hieronymian Martyrologium, but no faith can be placed in the attempts that have been made to fill up any of the details of his history. For example, it is now generally admitted that St. George cannot safely be identified by the nameless martyr spoken of by Eusebius (Church History VIII.5), who tore down Diocletian's edict of persecution at Nicomedia. The version of the legend in which Diocletian appears as persecutor is not primitive. Diocletian is only a rationalized form of the name Dadianus. Moreover, the connection of the saint's name with Nicomedia is inconsistent with the early cultus at Diospolis. Still less is St. George to be considered, as suggested by Gibbon, Vetter, and others, a legendary double of the disreputable bishop, George of Cappadocia, the Arian opponent of St. Athanasius."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-22"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-22">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFAttwater1995" class="citation book cs1">Attwater, Donald (1995) [1965]. <i>Dictionary of Saints</i> (Third&#160;ed.). <li id="cite_note-23"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-23">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="/wiki/Edward_Gibbon" title="Edward Gibbon">Edward Gibbon</a>, <i><a href="/wiki/The_History_of_the_Decline_and_Fall_of_the_Roman_Empire" title="The History of the Decline and Fall of the Roman Empire">The History of the Decline and Fall of the Roman Empire</a></i>, 2:23:5</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Emerson-24"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Emerson_24-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFRichardsonMoser1996" class="citation cs2">Richardson, Robert D.; Moser, Barry, eds. (1996), <i>Emerson</i>, p.&#160;520, <q>George of Cappadocia&#160;... [held] the contract to supply the army with bacon&#160;... embraced <a href="/wiki/Arianism" title="Arianism">Arianism</a>&#160;... [and was] promoted&#160;... to the episcopal throne of <a href="/wiki/Alexandria" title="Alexandria">Alexandria</a>&#160;... When Julian came, George was dragged to prison, the prison was burst open by a mob, and George was lynched&#160;... [he] became in good time Saint George of England</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=Emerson&amp;rft.pages=520&amp;rft.date=1996&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ASaint+George" class="Z3988"></span>.</span>
